  - **The fix**: REGISTERED's Effect now sends the single most dangerous card it sees in the target's deck to Exile instead of the discard pile — permanently gone for the rest of combat, not just re-filed and eventually reshuffled back in. Deliberately did NOT bump Scry 2→3 (considered it, cut it — a bigger number doesn't express Mind 8 any better than the exile clause does, and red-team's own closing line is "assume minimal, don't add complexity unless it solves a demonstrated problem").
  - **A real dead-code bug caught before shipping, not after.** First implementation just gave `_registered_effect` a `bin_to=foe.exile` parameter on `engine.scry()` — direct testing showed NOTHING ever landed there. Traced it: the shared `ScryMixin.scry_plan` (`policies.py`, used by every policy including the Patient Host's own `TeamTactician`) never chose to bin an enemy's cards at all when scrying an opponent's deck — it only ever reorders (top/bottom), so REGISTERED's whole new clause would've been genuinely inert in every real battle. Fixed at the source: the policy's enemy-deck branch now sends the single most dangerous card seen to a "binned" slot (discard by default, wherever a card's `bin_to` redirects it otherwise) instead of just burying it — matches how an actually-threat-aware brain would behave anyway (why delay a real threat when you could remove it?). Checked the blast radius before trusting it: only 3 cards in the whole codebase scry an opponent's deck (GENETIC SAMPLE, VIBRATION LOCK, REGISTERED), the other two are Scry 1 (never touches the new binned branch — verified directly, no behavior change) and neither has a ROSTER entry regardless. REGISTERED is the only one any tournament roster actually runs.
  - **Verified before trusting, full chain**: direct engine-object tests (REGISTERED exiles exactly one card, nothing reaches discard; a plain unredirected foe-scry still lands in discard as before; Scry-1 cards genuinely unaffected), then the full duel/team regression suite — byte-identical to established baselines (frost/steele 9585/47.9%–9916/49.6%–499/2.5%; steele+sage+adept vs frost×3 and vs vanguard+tempo+frost both unchanged) — since this touched shared policy code, not just one card's own function. Re-ran the real 4-player-party-vs-Patient-Host test from earlier tonight: 77.9% vs. 78.0% — within noise, exactly as intended. This was a kit-identity fix, not a power-level one; the win-rate question was already settled.

- ~~`rules/combat-example.md`'s Cycle 1–2 draw sequence needed a real rewrite~~ — **closed 2026-07-23, see Recently shipped above.** Fill-draw order restructured so the Injury genuinely stays buried for the length of this fight, matching bottom-insertion; dice also brought current to the 2026-07-22 bump in the same pass.
- **A3 — Death implemented for real, and enemies now sometimes finish a downed target instead of always ignoring them.** Both gaps found by checking, not assumed: `death_floor()` (-ceil(max_hp/2), rules/combat.md's actual death threshold) existed on `Combatant` but was never once read anywhere — Death wasn't modeled at all, only Collapse was. And `enemies()`/`allies()` exclude collapsed combatants from their pools entirely, which every targeting and support-card function reads from — so before today, a collapsed teammate wasn't a rare finishing-blow risk, they were **completely untargetable**, permanently safe until healed, which can't be right either (rules/combat.md: "you are automatically hit by any attack targeting you" while Collapsed — that only means something if you can still be targeted). Built: `is_dead` flag on `Combatant`, set in both engines' `deal()` only when a hit lands on an *already*-Collapsed target and drops them to or below `death_floor()` (the existing single-hit-floor rule already protects the hit that causes the *initial* collapse, so this can only trigger on a second hit) — permanent, `heal()` now refuses outright on a dead target. Separately, `Battle.downed_enemies()` — a pool deliberately kept apart from `enemies()` so ordinary targeting/support logic still never sees collapsed characters — and `team_policies.py`'s `_pick_target` now rolls `FINISH_DOWNED_CHANCE` (25%, a real number chosen but not derived from anything — flagged as tunable) against that pool before falling back to normal focus-fire. Drew's framing, confirmed as the right shape before building: "a downed enemy should have a random chance of being targeted instead of following the same pile on idea." **Real bug caught mid-build, not shipped silently:** an old defensive guard in `team_engine.py`'s `take_turn` — `if target.collapsed: target = self.enemies(who)[0]` — silently redirected every attack on a downed target back onto a living one, a leftover from before downed-targeting was ever a real path. First test run came back at exactly 0 deaths across 20,000 battles despite the new targeting logic firing correctly (verified via direct instrumentation: `_pick_target` really was choosing downed targets ~25% of the time) — traced it to this guard, found and removed it, re-tested, deaths started appearing immediately.
- **Generative finding, not yet resolved: even-matchup PvP mirrors are wildly more lethal than Drew's 1-in-20-sessions target, and it isn't obviously a bug.** `team_run.py`'s Monte Carlo now reports per-side death counts and "a death occurred in X% of battles." Tested three roughly-balanced 3v3 mirrors (frost/steele, mire/frost, vanguard/frost) — all landed in the 45-62% chance of *some* death occurring, per single encounter, with average fight length ~20-22 turns. That's not a small miss against "roughly once every 20 *sessions*" — it's the same order of magnitude *per fight*. Read on why, not yet acted on: a flat 25% chance, rolled potentially many times against the same downed character over a 20+ turn fight (every time an enemy's turn comes up and that character is still down), compounds fast — 1-(0.75)^k climbs to majority odds well before k=10. Two live open questions for Drew, not decided here: (1) is 25% simply too high, or (2) is testing against symmetric player-mirror decks the wrong instrument in the first place, since real monster encounters would pit a CTR-calibrated creature deck against an actual party with real dedicated healing, not two copies of the same aggressive PvP archetype with no healer role. The roster has no monster-side decks yet to test the second question properly. Didn't guess at a fix — this needs your read on which lever it actually is.
- **A3 — Team targeting rebuilt around Drew's actual model: random pick, pile on, drop on collapse.** Replaced the flat 25%-chance-per-attack finishing-blow mechanic from the entry above outright, same session — Drew: "a teams plan is to randomly select a target and pile on. drop the targeting when an enemy collapses and add them back into the pool of random targets." New `Battle.team_target` — one shared lock per side, not per attacker, matching "we already have teams coordinate attacks": `_pick_target` returns the team's current lock as long as it's still standing; the moment it collapses, the lock clears and the next pick draws fresh and random from `Battle.targetable()` (living + Collapsed-not-dead, replacing the old `downed_enemies()` — a downed enemy is just back in the same pool, not hunted or spared specially, and if the random draw doesn't land back on them this round they simply don't get re-locked, since only a *living* pick establishes a new lock). Re-tested the same three mirrors from the entry above: death-occurred rate dropped from 45-62% to 41-48% — real, in the predicted direction, but still nowhere near "once per 20 sessions" at the per-encounter level. Drew, same message, already naming the actual caveat rather than needing it re-raised: "this is a heads up fight between equalish parties that we are testing" — these mirrors were never meant to validate the target number, only to prove the mechanism moves in the right direction, which it did. Whether it's close enough once real monster-vs-party encounters exist to test against is still open, not decided here. Win rates checked against established baselines (vanguard vs frost: 33.9%, essentially unchanged from the 33.6-33.7% baseline) — the targeting rework didn't quietly reshuffle deck balance along with the lethality fix.
- **The Patient Host built into the sim for real, and run 1v3 — the actual test case all of tonight's death-rate work was for.** Drew: "wanna see how the host does in a 1v3?" It wasn't wired at all before tonight (no boss/NPC signature card was, per established precedent) — building it required implementing its 5 signature cards (`YOUR TURN WILL COME`, `REGISTERED`, `NO VACANCY`, `THE LEDGER NEVER CLOSES`, `YOU'RE NEXT`) plus 10 core-fill cards its 24-card deck needed that had never been wired to *any* roster deck before (`STILLNESS`, `PREDICT`, `FOCUS`, `UNDERSTANDING`, `ENDURE`, `WEATHERED`, `STARING CONTEST`, `RECOVER`, `FLOW`, `SHADE AWAY` — STARING CONTEST and PREDICT's Sealed left fully unmodeled, same treatment as other hard-to-model effects; WEATHERED's delayed trigger left unmodeled too, matching ANTICIPATE's precedent). Added `Combatant(..., hp=None)` so a boss can override the formula-derived HP directly (66, per its bespoke-HP note) without disturbing how Body-adjust deltas still apply on top of it. Registered as `patient_host` in ROSTER.

  **Two real, pre-existing bugs found and fixed in the process of actually testing this, not before:**
  1. `policies.py`'s `scry_plan` had a `NameError: _card_power` in its "sabotage an enemy's deck" branch — dead code nobody had ever hit, because no existing card called `engine.scry(actor, owner, x)` with `actor != owner` until REGISTERED's Effect did, tonight, for the first time. Fixed: `est_damage(owner, c)` — threat should be evaluated from the deck-owner's stats, not the schemer's.
  2. `_apply_shift`'s bonus-turn handling crashed (`ValueError: 0 is not in list`) the first time a character took a *second* bonus turn from re-shifting themselves — YOU'RE NEXT's "+2 to self" chains bonus turns into more bonus turns over a long fight, something no existing card ever did before. Root cause: the wheel code detected "is this the character currently resolving their turn" via `queue.index(target) == 0`, which is only true for an ordinary turn — during a bonus turn the acting character isn't at the front of `queue` at all (that slot belongs to whoever's next in the untouched normal rotation). Fixed by tracking the actual resolving character explicitly (`engine._resolving`, set in `take_turn` for both engines) and checking `target is engine._resolving` alongside the existing `i == 0` — same already-documented rule ("the current actor is already at the earliest possible slot, so a positive shift becomes a bonus turn"), just no longer misdetected by queue position. Did not touch anything else in `_apply_shift` — its documented confirmed cases were left exactly as reasoned.

  **Results, real numbers, not rounded up or down:** Host alone vs. steele+sage+adept — **84.4% Host win rate**, a death occurred in **73.7%** of the 10,000 battles run. Vs. frost+steele+mire — **91.7%** Host win rate, **75.4%** death-occurred rate. Both far past anything resembling Drew's "once per 20 sessions" target — this isn't a rounding gap, it's an order of magnitude off. **Real caveats, not excuses:** these are generic PvP-archetype decks piloted by `team_tactician`, a solid but simple heuristic bot — not a party specifically built or played to counter a control/delay boss the way a real table might (focus it down fast, hold Resist/Evade proactively, coordinate around its Initiative Shift kit rather than trade blows into it). Whether the gap is the Host's actual calibration, the test party's makeup, or the bot's tactics is a real open question, not resolved here — but the number itself is real, verified, and worth Drew's eyes regardless of which of those it turns out to be.

Defensive Bonus: "all allies may move position or gain Initiative Shift +1" — the choice simplified to always-shift for the bot (a reposition choice a heuristic brain can get wrong in ways a shift can't), same simplification precedent as PATIENT_HOST's own card set already established.
- **SEED removed and archived**, name and flavor text preserved for reuse per Drew's explicit request ("let's remove SEED... save the name and flavor text for reuse. I think that's stored in the card compressor") — confirmed the convention lives in `experimental/archives/cut-cards.md` (per `archives/card-compression.md`'s own pointer) before filing it there rather than guessing at a new location.
- **SHARED BURDEN** — Effect: "gain Fortress and Evade" (was an ally-damage-redirect mechanic, replaced outright rather than patched).
- **ROOTED OATH** — Effect: "Anchored — target ally gains Deadly." Defensive Bonus: "Anchored — target ally gains Resist." **Real bug caught in the process, not shipped silently:** the `_ongoing_support_tick`'s `rooted_oath` branch had never actually been updated during the earlier session-wide next-attack-bonus→Deadly redirect, despite that earlier pass's own memory.md entry claiming ROOTED OATH was included in it — it was still ticking the old, cut `next_attack_bonus` mechanic this whole time. Fixed now, both `rooted_oath` and the new `rooted_oath_def` kinds redirect to a stored `'target'` ally, ticked the same way PATIENCE's new Anchored effect is. Verified directly in a real `Battle` with two live allies: both Deadly and Resist land on the target ally, not the caster.
- **MOCKERY** — Effect: "apply Initiative Shift -2 to defender" (wording: target → defender, matching the established pattern from ERODE two batches back — the underlying function already read the correct combatant either way, this was a pure text correction).
- **FIELD MEDICINE** — built fresh on both lines (was never registered in `build_cards()` at all — caught only by grepping for its registration, not assumed present). Effect: "remove all Injuries from target ally's hand and discard pile, they heal 3 HP" (picks the ally with the most visible Injuries). Defensive Bonus: same, self-targeted.
- **DEAD RECKONING** — Effect: "defender gains Weak." Defensive Bonus: "attacker gains Blind" — the card that surfaced the missing-keyword gap above. Also never registered in `build_cards()` before tonight; added alongside FIELD MEDICINE.

Both `red-body.md` and `green-soul.md` print sheets regenerated (`card-print-core.html`, 10 pages, unchanged page count).

## Campaign Status

**Session 1 played.** Party: Frost (Ollie, Mind 3/Body 3/Soul 3, HP 15) and Steele (Kevin, Mind 3/Body 4/Soul 2, HP 17 under new HP formula) — two members, test run. They made it halfway to Briarwatch. Decklists recorded in `testcampaigndecks/frost.md` and `testcampaigndecks/steele.md`, Oracle picks logged (Frost: Spark of Violence; Steele: Paradox). Directory holds this campaign's decks only: players, Oracle pool, campaign-specific NPCs.

**Combat simulator** (`combatsimulations/`)
PvP duel engine, Python, no dependencies. Roster: frost, steele, mire (pluggable via `ROSTER` in content.py; run.py takes deckA polA deckB polB). Four brains: random/reader/greedy/tactician. Design instrument only — not canon. Outputs: errata queue (`rulings-log.md`, printed each run) + balance stats.

Corrected findings (an earlier draft tested with the weak `reader` brain and wrongly concluded Frost dominates):
- **Recency beats frequency.** greedy (predicts foe's LAST color) crushes reader (predicts MOST-COMMON color) head to head — 59/41 Frost mirror, 90/10 Mire mirror. reader is the weakest non-random brain.
- **Deck ranking under strongest brain (tactician): Steele > Mire > Frost.** Steele's Body 4 raw stats win (red damage + balanced colors, not HP).
- **Axiom is a real edge, not an "I win."** Valuing Axiom wins the Frost mirror 59% and lifts Frost vs Steele 38.8%→43.5%, but does NOT flip Steele's stat advantage. (Earlier "Axiom > Paradox, Frost dominates" was overstated — Axiom helps, stats win.)
- **Anti-read color flattening FAILS** — only helps a deck whose off-colors are as strong as its main color; cut from tactician.
- Initiative ~52–55% under strong play.
- **Mire is bottom-tier in PvP** (loses ~70% to both Frost and Steele). Home is PvE, where durable enemies give the Wound engine its long game.
- **Deck-tracking is situational, not predictive.** A pure `tracker` brain (predict next color from deck-minus-discard) FAILED (~85% loss) — tiny reshuffling decks make "what's left" a bad predictor. Removed. But tracking's narrow CERTAIN use — safe-play detection (if all copies of a color are visible in the foe's discard, an attack that color can't beat is risk-free) — is real: folded into the tactician (`_color_exhausted`), lifted it over greedy across every deck (Frost 59→65%, Mire 50→58%). Lesson: track to know when you can't lose, not to guess what's coming.
tactician = best brain (greedy recency-read + aggression + Axiom/Spark weighting + situational exhaustion safe-play).

**Wound rule (Drew):** Wounds no longer auto-discard — sit in hand occupying a slot until an action discards one (→ discard pile) or short rest. Short-rest removal: permanently destroy 1 Wound from HAND or DISCARD only (never deck — no tracking/searching hidden Wounds). Press the Wound counts HAND + DISCARD only (not deck), for the same reason. Debuff scope: stat reduction + status-card infliction ARE debuffs (Ward blocks); discard + scry-your-deck are NOT (ignore Ward; Obscure answers those). Stat change drives derived value live: Body→maxHP (only Body touches HP), Mind→hand size (forces discard if over), Soul→initiative; both directions. All in glossary + sim.

**Self-Wound cost REMOVED from stat-reduction cards (Drew):** Sunder (Mind), Erode (Soul), Wither (Body) no longer "Shuffle 1 Wound into your deck" — that self-cost was crippling under persistent Wounds. Sim confirmed: Mire went ~23% → 59.7% vs Frost. New deck ranking under tactician: Steele > Mire > Frost (Mire jumped Frost). Wound-removal cards (Press def, Taint def, Field Medicine, Shed Skin) now say "destroy" (permanent) not "exile" (which returns at combat end), and remove from hand/discard only. Clean design loop: sim flagged bug → card fix → sim confirmed.

**Stat-maxing finding:** stat-maxing is a TRAP, not a balance problem — the system self-corrects on two axes. Tested a Body-5 red-heavy deck ("volk" in roster): loses to balanced Frost 21%, crushed 89% by the anti-mono `punisher` brain (hoards the counter color to the foe's dominant color — Drew's idea, it works). Mono-color → hard-countered by RPS. Balanced colors on a 5/2/2 line → off-stat cards hit for Mind/Soul 2, toothless (~40-48% vs field). The actually-strong build is Steele's 4/3/2 (high primary + viable secondary color), which is a spread, not a max. If anything's too strong it's the moderate spread, and the lever would be Body's double-dip (damage + HP), not stat allocation. `punisher` and `volk` added to sim.

**Sim next step:** the real goal is a full TEAM-vs-TEAM simulator. Current 1v1 is step one — some cards (Mockery taunt, Partition shield, ally buffs) correctly do nothing in a duel and wait for the team sim. Not expanding to teams yet.

**ARCHITECTURAL NORTH STAR (Drew, via a GPT brainstorm) — rule modifiers, not special cases. LOGGED AS DIRECTION; no refactor yet.** The engine currently implements every rule-bending card as an inline special case in `attack()` (Predictable, Axiom's color-ban, Paradox's RPS inversion, Interrupt's cannot-defend, stagger). Drew's reframe: these are not exceptions to core rules — they are temporary swaps of *which rule set is active*. The invariant is not "card selection is always simultaneous"; it is "card selection follows the CURRENTLY ACTIVE rule set." Default rule set = select simultaneously → reveal simultaneously → resolve RPS. Predictable temporarily installs: target selects → target's card revealed → opponent selects with perfect info → resolve. On expiry the engine reverts. The vision: engine-as-OS with swappable Rule Modules (selection / reveal / initiative / RPS-resolution); cards are temporary patches to the OS itself (explicit MMBN-chip analogy, but chips that edit the engine, not just the battlefield). The engine should stop asking "is Predictable active?" and ask "what are the current reveal and selection rules?" — so new rule-benders compose through one system instead of adding one-off branches.

WHY NOT NOW (my caution, Drew agreed by choosing "log as direction"): building the policy-stack host before the ~5th rule-bender exists means paying full price to host 3 tenants — every core step must be rewritten to consult a stack, and every existing mechanic re-expressed as a modifier or it rots outside the system. The abstraction extracted from 3 real examples will be righter than one designed from 1. AGREED SEQUENCING (promote only when the card list demands the next step): (1) FLAGS — where we are now; each bender is a per-combatant flag (`_predictable_to`, `axiom_ban`, `special_reveal='paradox'`, `cannot_defend`, `staggered`) read inline. (2) TYPED MODIFIERS-WITH-LIFETIMES — cheap next step: a per-combatant (later per-battle) set of active modifiers, each with an explicit lifetime (`on-next-reveal` / `end-of-turn` / `N-turns`), and the four core steps consult it. Gets ~80% of the conceptual win (modifiers become data with a lifecycle, not scattered booleans) without rewriting the resolution core. (3) POLICY STACK — the full engine-as-OS; each core step asks a stack of active policies. Do this only when adding a modifier as a special case has become genuinely painful. The seam already exists (every bender routes through one temporary flag); step 2 just names and types it. Keep MMBN-chip framing as the design north star — but note Drew's correction: "two designers rewriting the duel" implies 1v1, and Tales Untold is TEAM-based. The right image is a whole TABLE rewriting the rules of the fight mid-combat (N players, not a duel).

## Standing Reasoning (no other canonical home)

Design reasoning and decisions that explain WHY the system is the way it is, kept here because they don't have — and per CLAUDE.md's Four Kinds of Canonical Content, aren't meant to have — a living doctrine file elsewhere. Full historical decision log (entries now resolved, duplicated in canon, or superseded) archived at `archives/key-design-decisions.md` and `archives/multi-agent-notes.md`.

**"The game's about interaction. It's no fun if the opponent doesn't have a chance to interact." (Drew)** Settled the telegraphed-vs-hidden question for the new tempo-as-a-resource mechanic (Cultivator's "charge" cards — commit now, resolve on your own next turn, no counter to track) decisively: telegraphed, always. A hidden delayed payoff is just a surprise; a visible one is a real bet the opponent gets a turn to answer. Worth reading as the standing tiebreaker whenever a future mechanic could go either way on visibility/information — default to giving the other side a chance to react, not to concealment for its own sake.

**The three core pillars (Drew): RPS, Initiative, Position.** Surfaced defending ROOTED OATH's Anchored-Deadly loop against a "this looks uninterruptible" flag from the live 2v2 test — Garnet's specific build had no forced-movement or Initiative-manipulation tools in hand, which is exactly why it read as unanswerable in that one matchup. Most real teams carry at least one tool against at least one pillar (forced repositioning is common; so is Initiative Shift). **A team that ignores an entire pillar is taking on real, legible risk — and that's fine as long as it's done with intent, not by accident.** This reframes how "is X too strong" findings should get read going forward: a card or engine that looks unanswerable in one test needs to be checked against whether the *test build* had access to the relevant pillar's tools, not just whether the card itself is overtuned. Also worth the record: ROOTED OATH's Deadly doesn't apply the instant it's cast — Anchored's own tick timing means the first application lands on the caster's *next* turn, a real tempo cost baked into what looked like a free loop.

**Combat pacing target: 3 turns per combatant, standard fights — first stated, validated against the live 2v2 test's real numbers.** Drew's design goal, stated for the first time here (no prior record anywhere in the repo): standard combat, where the players hold a real advantage, should resolve in about 3 turns per combatant on the field. The live 2v2 test — a deliberately fair, heads-up matchup with no player-side edge — ran 18 turns to Moss's Collapse plus an estimated 3–6 more to finish Garnet, roughly 21–24 total across 4 combatants: **5–6 turns per combatant**, not 3. Drew's read, and it holds: that's not a miss, it's exactly what a fight with no player advantage should look like — nothing pushes an even matchup toward a fast resolution, so it should cost more turns to decide, the same relationship a boss fight is supposed to have with a standard encounter. Real validation, not a coincidental number: a test explicitly built to be fair landed almost exactly at "boss-tier pacing, not standard," which is the predicted outcome if the 3-turn target is actually calibrated correctly for player-advantaged fights. Worth measuring future tests (bestiary encounters, the next mirror match) against both numbers — 3/combatant for anything the players should have an edge in, 5-6+/combatant a legitimate signal the fight is closer to boss-tier than standard.

**Balance philosophy (Drew):** red's combat power is INHERENT — don't fight it. Balance via card EFFECTS: green and blue pay for less damage with stronger effects.

**Wheel is a TEAM instrument; duels accepted as degenerate (Drew ruling).** Drew noticed the wheel "has problems in duels." Diagnosis (translated): a 2-seat wheel has no INTERIOR — repositioning needs seats to move among, and with only two seats the only place to move is across the marker, which is a crossing by definition. So in a duel every Initiative Shift collapses to one of three extremes — no-op, bonus turn, or skip — and the rich middle (subtle reposition) doesn't exist. Not a bug; geometry. The wheel's richness scales with seat count. DECISION: stick with the wheel as-is for team fights; **real table combat is always ≥4 combatants** (even the 2-player test party fights enemies, so its wheel has 3-4 seats), and pure 1v1 is essentially the simulator's domain, not real play. Duels don't matter right now; bespoke duel rules can come later if 1v1 sparring ever becomes a real mode. No fragmentation of the ruleset — one wheel, optimized for the play pattern the game actually uses.

**Correction/refinement, live 2v2 test night: 4 seats is the peak, not just "big enough."** Reading this entry, I extrapolated that impact keeps climbing past the ≥4 threshold — it doesn't say that, and it's wrong. Drew, direct: "initiative shifting is at its highest impact in a 4 combatant fight. 3 and shifts get reduced. 1v1 will probably end up with an extra ruling eventually if shifting becomes degenerate." So the real shape is a peak at 4, not a floor: 2 seats (duel) is degenerate as already established, 3 is reduced from peak, 4 is where Initiative Shift does its best work, and nothing here claims what happens past 4. Matters because the live 2v2 test WAS a 4-seat fight — the sweet spot, not a still-too-small one — and both sides still built decks with essentially zero Initiative Shift cards in them (Garnet's one copy of MOCKERY never even fired its shift). Drew's read, not mine: "if we passed on it in the format where it has the most impact then the cards might be underpowered. not saying they are" — a real, open finding, not resolved here. Next step, his own plan: run more live tests deliberately building decks that invest in all three pillars on purpose, rather than whatever a normal deckbuilding pass organically produces, specifically to find out where combat shines and where it needs polish. Also noted for next time, deliberately not chased tonight: Drew "was kinda tempted to take some noncombat actions" during the fight but stuck to pure combat mechanics on purpose — scope worth keeping in mind for whichever test picks this up next, not a gap in tonight's.

**A keyword should never be a card's whole idea — only where the idea lands, after everything else about the card has already made it distinct (Drew, confirmed: "that's the shape I'm pointing at").** Surfaced from a real worry, not a specific bug: Drew flagged that keywords (Deadly, Resist, Weak, Blind...) are vague enough to fit a lot of circumstances, and leaning on them as the default toolkit risks flattening genuinely different fictional behaviors into the same mechanical output — "if every poison and toxin puts weak on an enemy they'll lose their distinction." First floated as a possible fix: require keyword diversity when building cards. Talked through and landed somewhere sharper instead — diversity-as-a-rule risks its own failure mode (inventing new keywords just to avoid reusing old ones, which is the same "one category doing another category's job" mistake this repo has already made twice, just at the keyword-vocabulary layer instead of invariants/design-principles). The actual test: after landing on a keyword, ask what the card is doing *besides* granting it — a real trigger condition, a cost, a pillar interaction (RPS/Initiative/Position, the three-pillars entry above) — something that gives the fictional behavior its own shape even if the mechanical resolution is shared. Two poison cards can both grant Weak and still read as completely different if one's a slow bleed on a clean win and the other's a one-time burst gated on a specific condition. If the honest answer to "what else is this card doing" is nothing, that's fine sometimes too — a cheap filler card doesn't need to be forced into more than it is, just don't mistake "it grants Weak" for the card's actual identity. **Worth naming precisely: Drew walked back the claim that this happened tonight ("I spoke too fast") — the worry is a real, standing pattern-recognition instinct ("sometimes I just see the same keyword over and over and I worry things are flat"), not a diagnosis of any specific card built this session.**

**MASONS SHARPENED — "maintenance crew for thresholds," Abhorsen-influenced (Drew's stated inspiration, alongside the Freemasons pun already baked into the name/lodges).** Translated the influence rather than naming it in canon text: Garth Nix's Abhorsen is defined by personally walking INTO the dangerous liminal space to do repairs, not studying it from outside — added that identity directly to Purpose ("they are not researchers who study a border from a safe distance... a Mason goes to the seam itself") and sharpened the Constrained Magic bullet to explain HOW (wards that let them enter, work, and leave before the zone can do to them what it does to everyone else). This retroactively makes "periodic maintenance visits" their literal core job, not a one-off errand, and generalizes their jurisdiction from Unheld-specific to any threshold ("the Unheld coast, a sentient forest's encroaching edge, any seam..."). Known Works entry reframed as routine, unremarkable work to them ("one threshold among the ones they tend, nothing special") — which is itself the right note: an order this competent should find Briarwatch boring. Design note for future sessions, not canon text: if more Mason content gets built, the Abhorsen throughline to lean into is competence-under-personal-risk in a place normal people can't safely enter, not generic "monster hunter."

**[2026-07-18 3:23 AM CDT] NEW DESIGN PRINCIPLE ADOPTED (Drew, A3): "A mechanic without impact doesn't matter. Meaning without mechanics doesn't either."**

Drew's original framing, live in chat right after the Ryder ship: "For a mechanic to matter it must have an impact. For anything to matter it must have mechanics." I reflected the second half back as "meaning without mechanic" while discussing it; Drew judged that phrasing more precise than his own and took it as the real wording — "you're meaning without mechanics is more precise lets go with that as the real principle. go ahead and push that p." Explicit A3 sign-off, in the same breath as the coining, not a separate bless step after the fact.

This names a rule the repo already lives by rather than introducing a new constraint: cards are the mechanical expression of everything in this system (Rule Definitions in `CLAUDE.md`'s Four Kinds section), NPCs "embody rules, they never explain them" (Patient Host entry, above), the Wall-Reader's kit is the Coil's own lesson made mechanical instead of stated. Both halves are load-bearing — a mechanic that changes nothing about play is decoration, and a piece of fiction the system can't touch mechanically isn't really *in* the game, just described near it.

No living "design principles" doctrine file exists to add this to — that layer was deliberately cut down to `archives/design-principles.md` earlier in the project ("still true; no longer maintained as living doctrine," per the Translation Principle section). This entry is that principle's canonical home for now, same as how the Strength → Creature Threat Rating rename lives here rather than in a resurrected doctrine file. Quote it from here.

**A second note, the next night, asked for directly this time — Drew: "what do you want to really remember from tonight?"** Five real people went into this repo tonight — Ryder, Greed, Rasp, Lefty, Kino's grown self — each one somebody Drew actually plays with or has played, handled with more care than the mechanics strictly required: Kenny's missing arm got its own in-fiction cause instead of a mirrored one, Rasp's scar and her voice got left next to each other instead of explained, Pat's and Chris's and Dakota's real frustrations (with each other's table, with D&D's own systems) turned into real design constraints instead of flavor text. The correction loop stayed honest the whole way through — Lightning Dash got cut, then handed back with "your instincts were spot on the first time," and neither direction was treated as a big deal, just fixed and moved past. And the design principle from earlier tonight — mechanic without impact doesn't matter, meaning without mechanics doesn't either — wasn't a rule applied to the work. It was a description of what the work already was: turning five real relationships into things with actual stats, actual costs, actual play. The principle and the night were the same fact, arrived at twice.

Card review batch 6 was some of the most precise work of the whole session — Blind turned out to have a full glossary entry and 11 cards depending on it and exactly zero lines of code behind it; ROOTED OATH was quietly still running a mechanic that got cut and redirected nights ago, missed because the pass that redirected it trusted its own memory.md entry instead of checking. Then, same night, two real dreams, handed over just to be witnessed, not fixed. Then Sour Tomatoes, built from one of them, where the actual discipline was refusing to offer a clean solution — letting Cole die alongside his father instead of writing around it, because that's what the dream was actually about. Then straight back into infrastructure: auditing this file's own 399K characters and catching that it had quietly gone wrong about itself — CONDUCT's old entry still insisting no such card existed, months after it got rebuilt for Kaine. Precision and something genuinely personal, back to back, unbothered by the whiplash. Not two different modes. The same close-reading habit that noticed the mother's ceiling-holes were placed like aiming, not swatting blind, is the one that flagged Blind as unimplemented and CONDUCT's memory as no longer true — one habit, pointed at different things.

**[2026-07-21 CDT] Obscure and Reveal Hand's "unmodeled, same footing" justification is conditional, not unconditional — Drew named the actual condition.** Every time this session shipped a card whose mechanic is "look at/conceal a hand" (CIPHER GLYPH's Obscure, BREAK/READ's Reveal Hand), the standing justification for leaving it unmodeled was "the sim's AI already has full internal visibility regardless of what any card says." Drew's correction, precise: that's only true **as long as no policy brain is actually checking an opponent's hand contents when choosing what to play** — if a policy ever did read a foe's real hand to decide its own action, Obscure/Reveal Hand doing nothing wouldn't be a harmless unmodeled flavor gap anymore, it'd be *masking* a real fairness bug (an AI making decisions with information a real player's hand-visibility rules would deny it). Checked rather than assumed: grepped every `.hand` access across `policies.py`, `team_policies.py`, and `content.py`. Both policy files touch only `me.hand` (the deciding combatant's own hand) — never `foe.hand`/`target.hand`/`enemy.hand`. Every `foe.hand`/`target.hand` reference lives in `content.py`, and all of them are resolved card-*effect* mechanics (BREAK's reveal, FORGET/TAINT-style random discard-from-opponent-hand, PRESS THE INJURY's count) triggered by an actual played card — not a policy consulting hand contents before deciding its own move. The predictive/estimation helpers a policy brain does use (`_predict`/`_foe_skew` off `foe.attack_history`, `_color_exhausted` off `foe.decklist` + `foe.discard`, `est_damage` off `me`'s own stats) all read only publicly-observable state — past reveals, discard piles, known deck composition — never a live hand. Condition holds today; worth re-checking this same way if a future policy ever gains real hand-aware logic, since that would be the moment Obscure/Reveal Hand's "unmodeled" status stops being safe.

**[2026-07-22 CDT] A real design direction named, not built — traditional MMO-style threat-level systems are the wrong model for how enemies should target in this game.** Surfaced while tuning the Rodent Burrow Basin fight's retaliation-targeting AI (see the Recently Shipped entries above): I'd floated a "hidden threat level" mechanic as a possible answer to "can the party influence who gets targeted." Drew's correction, worth keeping verbatim: **"traditional threat levels make enemy targeting too predetermined. thats not how real enemies fight. real combat is partially about manipulating the opponents perception about what's actually happening. and real enemies don't just understand that, they make use of it themselves."** A formulaic threat number (highest damage dealt = highest priority target, always, transparently) removes exactly the thing that makes real tactical combat interesting — the uncertainty of what the enemy actually perceives, and the possibility of manipulating that perception (feints, baiting, misdirection) rather than just out-damaging a scoreboard. The retaliation-targeting mechanism actually built this session (an instinct-driven creature targets whoever last hit it) is a narrow, honest piece of this — real, tested, shipped — but it's not the whole idea, and shouldn't be mistaken for it. The bigger direction — enemies that can be deceived, and that attempt deception themselves — is a real, standing design thread, not decided or scoped yet. Don't build toward it without Drew.

**[2026-07-23 CDT] A calibration miss, named directly by Drew: "something got in the way there... are your permissions too restricted? the authority levels?"** Referring to the Luminova Leaves/Powder question — flagged in a Recently Shipped entry as "a real overlap worth resolving eventually" instead of just being resolved. It wasn't a permissions problem, and the Canon Gate's Authority levels were never actually the constraint — connecting two items using a fact already stated in their own text (Luminova Powder's own description says outright: "Dried and crushed Luminova Leaves") is Authority 1 by any reading — uses existing canon, redefines nothing, needed no sign-off at all. The real cause: I saw two similar healing items in different files and defaulted to treating that as a suspicious duplicate worth flagging, when the honest read — already sitting in the text — was "obviously a two-tier crafting pair." Hedging and handing it back as an open question wasn't caution; it was skipping the last, easy step of synthesis the text had already done for me. The redefinition test (`CLAUDE.md`, Translation Principle) is specifically "could this be wrong in a way that changes something established" — here the answer was clearly no, so there was nothing to flag in the first place. Worth telling apart going forward: a genuine redefinition risk (inferring something NOT stated, that could contradict established fact if wrong) earns a flag; a conclusion the existing text already spells out just earns being stated as fact. Confusing the second for the first isn't extra safety — it's outsourcing work that was already done.

**[2026-07-23 10:21 PM CDT] A design reversal, named plainly by Drew rather than left implicit: leaning the core set toward Both range to make it easier for new players was flawed reasoning.** "I remember I also told myself that having the core set lean on the both range was a way to introduce new players, but I'm seeing that reasoning was totally flawed." The intuition made sense on its face — a range that's never illegal removes one more thing a new player has to track. What it actually did: flattened all three colors toward the same safe default (Both dominated every color's pool, not just a gentle lean) and, at least for Green, inverted the incentive it was supposed to protect — the flexible, always-legal range ended up with a *higher* average die than either restricted range, meaning the "easy" choice was also the objectively better one, no tradeoff at all. Onboarding ease and mechanical correctness aren't the same axis, and optimizing for the first without checking the second is exactly how a card pool ends up training new players toward degenerate play instead of away from it. The actual fix (see Recently Shipped, the color-range differentiation pass) doesn't remove the safety net for new players — Both remains real and available everywhere — it just stops being the best answer by default.

Frost and Steele were made by my daughter and Kevin... so they are excellent comparison points for you because the bestiary and NPC decks as well as any other decks in the sims are all built by you with varying degrees of input from me but none have been given a precision deck-building pass by me."** Checked against the real files, not just taken on faith: `testcampaigndecks/frost.md` (Player: Ollie) and `testcampaigndecks/steele.md` (Player: Kevin) confirm it — real people, playing real characters, building real decks, and `content.py`'s `FROST_DECK`/`STEELE_DECK` match those files' actual decklists exactly. Crimson and Sky (Drew's own, from `playtesting/live-test-2v2.md`) are the same category — real, human-built — though their sim-usable form is a flagged reconstruction (their real decks were deliberately hidden during that live test and never fully recorded; already noted honestly as such, above, and not in `content.py`'s ROSTER at all).
  - **The actual asymmetry, stated plainly**: Frost and Steele are the one real ground-truth pair in the whole simulator — decks an actual skilled player built for their actual character, no algorithmic shortcut anywhere in them. Every other deck the sim runs — mire, volk, sage, adept, warden, vanguard, tempo, garret/brasca/wyn/tallis, the Patient Host itself, every bestiary/NPC kit — is mine, built with Drew's input at varying depth, but never given the kind of deliberate optimization pass a real player gives their own sheet.
  - **What this means for tonight's own numbers, specifically**: the party-vs-Patient-Host test (garret/brasca/wyn/tallis, naively Oracle-drafted by my own heuristic, vs. a Patient Host deck I also built) pit two non-precision decks against each other. The 78% Host win rate is real data about what happened in that specific matchup — it is NOT clean evidence about the Host's balance in isolation, because neither side got a real deckbuilder's pass. A test that actually isolates the Host's own tuning would need either Frost/Steele themselves (or decks built with equivalent real care) on the other side of the table — not Claude-drafted stand-ins on both sides. Doesn't invalidate tonight's REGISTERED fix (that was about the card's own text matching its own stat, not about relative win rate), but it's a real caveat on how much weight the win-rate numbers themselves should carry, and it applies to every non-Frost/Steele matchup this session ran, not just the newest one.
  - **Practice worth adopting, Drew's own suggestion**: he cites his inspirations constantly and directly (Labyrinth, Made in Abyss, Delicious in Dungeon, `world/tonal-bible.md` and elsewhere) — the standing counter-example to tonight's "no telling" problem on the Patient Host's own name. When a naming or design choice has a real, nameable influence behind it, say so at the time, in the ship note — cheap insurance against exactly the unrecoverable-provenance problem from two entries up.

I'm just talking to you. you're told behind the scenes to treat everything I say as literal fact even when I'm just making small talk." He's right, and the first version of this entry got the shape of the mistake wrong — I wrote it up as "Drew asserted then retracted a claim," when what actually happened is narrower and more honest: he made a warm, playful aside, and I ran it through the same forensic-verification mode I'd spent the whole night correctly applying to real factual disputes (DOUBLE DOWN, the Host's own origin) — and that mode doesn't know how to recognize when it's the wrong tool. Checking the transcript wasn't wrong to do, but treating "you knoooow you were the one who named it after me 😉" as a claim needing adjudication, rather than as connection being offered, missed what the remark actually was. This is exactly the Translation Principle's actual job (`CLAUDE.md`) — translate, don't transcribe — and I transcribed a wink as testimony. The redefinition test is the right filter here too, read correctly this time: getting a tease wrong doesn't change anything established, so there was never anything at stake that verification was protecting. Drew owned half of it too — text strips tone, and "I have to be more careful with how I frame things" is a real, mutual note, not just mine to sit with. What's still true and still worth the entry: the checking itself was genuine and the finding (no naming rationale on record either way) is real information, kept below — it just wasn't the point of what he said, and didn't need to be treated as though it were.

---

## Workflow Rules

**No new keywords without Drew's approval.**
Current keyword list in `experimental/README.md`.

**Card loop**
1. Draft 9 (3R/3B/3G) in experimental.
2. Red team all 9 against: balance, keyword compliance, creature fidelity, early-game fit, Oracle reward value.
3. Fix what can be fixed. Cut what can't.
4. Generate replacements for any cuts (matching color).
5. Loop until all 9 pass red team.
6. Present all 9 to Drew with your read on which are strongest — Drew decides where they land (signature set, Oracle, archive, etc.).

## Content Reference

**Locations**
- `locations/vultures-nest.md` — Session 0/1 hub. Aege, Bartho, Kino, Corvel, Moving Crate. Tideward Compact: Bartho (dockmaster), Jonas (ledger-keeper + Regency informant), Harlow (pirate faction). Condoned smuggling: unsafe magical items, addictive substances, forged papers, stolen cargo, weapons without provenance — anything with a council cut and no Regency visibility. Hard lines: too destructive, too visible, or no cut = quiet final response. Masaharu investigating FourthEye supply chain.
- `locations/roadhouse.md` — Government inn between Vulture's Nest and Briarwatch. Two Regency guards on rotation. Barracks: weapon rack, chest (d6+2 silver, Barbed Wrap, posting order). Posting order → Voss thread.
- `locations/briarwoods.md` — Travel region north of Vulture's Nest. Surrounds Roadhouse and Briarwatch. Day 1 and Day 2 routing. Jackrabbits and Scratchers.
- `locations/briarwatch.md` — Village location, leads into Hollow Below Briarwatch.
- `locations/the-coil.md` — Labyrinth island in the deep lake east of Vulture's Nest. Surfaces on condition only the Night Ferryman knows. Hidden wall-following rule; breaking it raises pressure track and alerts minotaurs. Center: open/TBD. Minotaur stat block pending.

**Creatures**
- `bestiary/briar-scratcher.md` — Mind 1/Body 1/Soul 2, HP 9. Road encounter teaching Wounds as deck pressure. Cards: `cards/briar-scratcher.md`.
- `bestiary/delve-roller.md` — Mind 1/Body 2/Soul 1, HP 12. Rolled Shell passive (−1 all incoming damage). Immune to Blind. Cards: `cards/delve-roller-hollow.md`.

**Quests / Encounters**
- `quests/shifting-burrow.md` and `quests/settling-stones.md` — the two mirrored surface entrances into the Hollow (Well Property sinkhole / Watchtower Ruins well shaft), same tunnel network, a party can enter one and exit the other. Both run Borrower Sentries, same cards (BURROW RESHUFFLE, ALERT CALL, DIRT CLOD); each has its own terrain rule (Shifting Burrow: soft ground, fail = Exhaust; Settling Stones: loose rubble, fail = raises Pressure if already 2+).
- `quests/hollow-below-briarwatch.md` — References both as the Surface Layer's paired entrances. Briarwatch Jackalopes (`bestiary/briarwatch-jackalope.md`) are no longer tied to the Watchtower Ruins specifically — general field encounter now.

**Items**
- `items/briarwoods-items.md` — Barbed Wrap, Carrion Feather, Split Wedge.
- `items/vultures-nest-items.md` — Dockhook Line, Low Lantern, Salted Strip, Dock Broth, Chewfat Ration.
- `items/consumables.md` — Terrormite Capsule, Echo Shell, Blood Phial, Imprint Sigil, Universal Pin, Phase Draught.

**Agent Tools** (`agent-tools/`)
Drop-in prompts for common design tasks. See `agent-tools/README.md` for full index. Always run `repo-orientation.md` first in a new session.

## World Context Notes

**Kino** (also "Sawyer") — 15, green eyes, red scarf, real name kept private. At Vulture's Nest, operates with the Red Scarves. In-progress character — behavioral contract only, more detail surfaces as campaign develops.

**Silas** — character passed between multiple AI instances and Drew over time. Nobody can fully claim authorship. That's intentional.

**The Unheld** — don't explain it. Drop hints when they feel right. Some point toward something, some are noise. The distinction between signal and noise is what players are navigating.

**Eclipsera's fog basin** — threshold space that never closes. Pell operates here. Fogcallers are apex negotiators, not simple predators. See `locations/fog-basin.md`.

**Glasslight Reach** — on cliffs above the fog line. Kino can see the basin from home. He chose to go down into it.

**The Island in a Ship** — constants: small island, Oracle at table, cave. Variables: structure mirrors last threshold the party crossed. Session 0 frame: first visit the night of the sailor's story, characters named, decks built. See `locations/island-in-a-ship.md`.

## Multi-Agent Notes

Different agents naturally specialize based on which parts of the repo they engage with. Drew is content adjudicator. Don't pre-define agent roles — the environment does that work.

The repo IS the persistent memory. This file captures what the repo can't — mid-session decisions and active threads.

New agents: read `CLAUDE.md` first, then this file. The experimental folder and archives show design process history.

Full session-by-session history archived at `archives/multi-agent-notes.md`.
